In \naddition, control locks shall be engraved with the division name and \"Control Lock.\" \n5.11 The following steps must be followed when implementing a group \nlockout box: \n As a job requiring a group lockout box is implemented, you must identify which energy \ncontrol and verification procedures are necessary. The energy control and verification \nprocedure will then be used to ensure the equipment is locked out and tried out correctly. \n5.11.1 Whenever a group lockout box lockout is implemented, a control lock shall be placed \non the group lockout box to control access to the equipment lock keys. The control \nlock shall be the first lock-on and the last lock-off. \n5.11.2 The group lockout leader will determine which authorized employee possesses the \ncontrol lock key (for the shift) for a group lockout box. Control lock keys shall not be left \nunsecured (e.g., in office drawers, etc.). \n5.11.3 The employee in possession of the control lock key is responsible for removing the \ncontrol lock when: \n The job is complete, and the post energy isolation activity checklist (CCS-HSS-\nFRM-0005.0) is filled out, or when, \n The lockout is being altered, requiring modification to the energy isolation \nverification procedure form or a new energy isolation. \n Whenever a lock box is opened and a key(s) removed, it needs to be noted on the \nenergy isolation verification procedure form immediately. \n5.11.4 If there are any alterations to an energy isolation activity related to the \nservicing/maintenance that affects the energy isolation and verification procedure (e.g., \ntesting, changes in what is isolated, and change in scope of servicing/maintenance):  \n All changes made to the previously locked and tried-out equipment must be \ndocumented on the energy isolation verification procedure (CCS-HSS-FRM-\n0001.0). \n All personnel affected by the energy isolation activity are notified through a Job \nSafety Briefing, TAKE 5, or JJHAC. \n All authorized employees re-applying their personal safety lock must re-verify that \nequipment that may affect their safety is locked out and tried out before returning to \nwork by at least reviewing the energy isolation and verification procedure. \n Do NOT assume you are still protected because you were protected when you first \nplaced your personal safety lock on the group lockout box. \n \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 26 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 26 of 52 \n5.11.5 Whenever a group lockout box lockout is implemented, and employees apply their \npersonal safety locks for protection, the Group Lockout Box Control List shall be \nutilized to document who and when employees are locked out on the group lockout \nbox and when employees remove their personal safety lock from the group lockout \nbox. \n5.11.6 Two people, at minimum, are required when using the group lockout box system. \n First, one authorized employee [A] shall place the equipment locks. \n Next, another employee [B] verifies the placement of the equipment lock(s) \naccording to the written energy isolation verification procedure. \n Then, either employee [A] or [B] shall ensure the equipment is tried out. \n Tryout can be accomplished through one or more of the following acts: \n Attempting to operate the equipment \n Visually verifying that needed safeguards are in place or that blades in the \ncorresponding disconnect box are disengaged \n Using a voltmeter or other test device \n Some lockout/tryout activities will require all three tryout methods to ensure \nthat energy sources are properly locked out. \n5.11.7 The identity of the equipment lock keys inside the group lockout box must be visible. \n5.11.8 A copy of the energy isolation verification procedure shall be placed outside the group \nlockout box in a document holder attached to the group lockout box for employee \nviewing.  \n5.11.9 During shift change, the oncoming authorized employees shall ensure that the group \nlockout box is still in effect before placing their personal safety lock(s) on the group \nlockout box and starting to work. \n5.11.10 Before removing the control lock of any lockout/tryout scenario and releasing the \nequipment to operations, verify all work is complete, and a post energy isolation \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 27 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 27 of 52 \nactivity checklist is completed and signed by a Cleveland-Cliffs Steel responsible \nManager. \n5.11.11 The completed post activity checklist (CCS-HSS-FRM-0001.0) must be returned to \nthe Health, Safety, and Security Department for auditing. \n5.11.12 Any time a personal safety lock or equipment lock is placed on an energy isolation \ndevice (e.g., disconnect switch, valve, pin, etc.), it shall be locked in a manner to \nprevent removal without the use of excessive force or unusual techniques.  \n5.11.13 Locks shall never be left on the energy isolation device, group lockout box, or lockout \nhasp while un-locked; they shall be placed and immediately locked.  \n5.11.14 Equipment locks shall be returned to the designated storage location while not being \nused for lockout. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 28 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 28 of 52 \n5.11.15 Keys for all equipment locks placed for lockout/tryout shall always be controlled. \nEquipment lock keys shall never be left unsecured in the group lockout box. \n5.11.16 Each energy isolation device on the equipment is secured with an assigned \nequipment lock by the Group Lockout Leader per the written energy isolation and  \nverification procedure. \n5.11.17 An authorized employee(s) places each equipment lock key in the group lockout box \nafter securing each equipment lock on the assigned energy isolation device.  \n5.11.18 An assigned control lock is applied to the group lockout box by the Group Lockout \nLeader. \n5.11.19 The assigned control lock is the first lock placed on a group lockout box and the last \nlock removed from the group lockout box. \n5.11.20 Each authorized employee places their personal safety lock on the group lockout box \nestablished for the equipment to be serviced. \n IMPORTANT: You must verify that you are placing your personal safety lock on \nthe correct group lockout box or boxes. \n5.11.21 When work requiring lockout has been completed, the authorized employees remove \ntheir personal safety lock from the group lockout box.  \n5.11.22 When energy isolation work is complete, the Group Lockout Leader completes a post \nactivity checklist (CCS-HSS-FRM-0005.0). \n5.11.23 Next, the Group Lockout Leader removes the control lock from the group lockout \nbox, freeing the keys to the equipment locks. \n5.11.24 For the Kirk Key and Castell Control Systems, follow the Risk Assessment, \nPracticality, and Justification requirements. \n5.12 Energy Control Verification Procedures \n5.12.1 Written energy control verification procedures shall be developed and maintained in \neach department.  \n5.12.2 Energy control verification procedures shall cover the steps to ensure that the serviced \nequipment is isolated and secured from all energy sources, including stored or residual \nenergy, which could injure personnel working on the equipment. \n5.12.3 Energy control verification procedures shall exist to lockout and try out machines, \nequipment, or operating units and be task specific. \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 29 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 29 of 52 \n5.12.4 When using an energy control and verification procedure not written explicitly for a \nservicing or maintenance job/task, all energy-isolation devices not needed to protect \nauthorized or affected employees shall be lined out and initialed on the energy \nisolation and verification procedure. \n5.13 Written energy control and verification procedures shall identify and \nprovide information on the following: \n5.13.1 A specific statement of the intended use of the procedures. \n5.13.2 Type of machinery or equipment being locked out. \n5.13.3 Types of energy sources and magnitude, such as electrical (480v), pneumatic (35 psi), \nhydraulic, steam, etc., must be locked out. \n5.13.4 Method and location of the lockout. \n5.13.5 Location(s) where tryout takes place. \n5.13.6 The stored or residual energy sources such as capacitors, springs, elevated machine \nmembers, rotating flywheels, pressure in hydraulic, pneumatic, or steam systems, etc., \nmust be controlled. \n5.13.7 Method and location to dissipate or restrain by grounding, repositioning, blocking, \npinning, bleeding down, etc. \n5.13.8 If applicable, the method and location of lockout of the dissipating or restraining \ndevice. \n5.13.9 Method and location where tryout will take place. \n5.13.10 The method of verification that shall be used to assure that a Zero Energy State has \nbeen achieved and maintained for the machinery or equipment being serviced. \n5.13.11 Lockout involving just one authorized individual for a single energy source requires a \n“Tryout” of the equipment but does not require a written energy control and \nverification procedure. \n5.14 Troubleshooting, Testing, or Positioning Equipment/Machines \n5.14.1 In situations where lockout devices must be removed temporarily from the energy \nisolating devices and the machine or equipment energized to troubleshoot, test, or \nposition the equipment or machine, the following must be observed: \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 30 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 30 of 52 \n Use three-part communication.  \n Survey and replace any missing machine guarding and clear the machine of tools and \nequipment that may interfere with its operation. \n Make certain controls are in the neutral or \"OFF\" position. \n All employees (affected, authorized, and others) are notified that the lockout devices will \nbe removed and the equipment reenergized. All employees shall be located outside of \nthe radius of movement of the equipment and in clear view of the point of control of the \nmachine area. \n Remove the lockout device(s) for the equipment sections to be checked. \n Energize and proceed with the equipment's troubleshooting, testing, or positioning only \nwhile directly communicating with all the above employees. \n When finished, shut down the equipment or machine, de-energize all energy sources, \nreplace the lockout devices, and test operating controls. \n Document the changes to the energy control and verification procedure immediately. \n \n CAUTION: Automatic equipment has a built-in memory and, in many instances, \nmay cycle or be activated when power is restored to equipment. \n5.14.2 Where equipment must be operated at intervals to complete repair work, energy \ncontrol and verification procedures must be established to remove and replace lockout \ndevices. \n5.14.3 There must be three-part communications between the persons removing the lock and \noperating the equipment and those at the equipment who may be aligning or \nperforming other work in conjunction with the repair. \n5.15 Energy control of Piping Systems \n5.15.1 Before any piping system is opened, energy control and verification procedures must \nbe established to safely reduce to a zero-energy state and verify that this has been \ndone for the section being worked on. \n5.15.2 System shut down to zero energy state is always preferred. However, if the work to be \ndone will affect the integrity of the system valves, blanks, blinds, or other controls, the \nsystem must be at zero energy. \n5.15.3 Blank, Blind, or bolted slip blind is acceptable if there has been no failure under the \nsame or like conditions. \n5.15.4 Conditions for the use of Double Block and Bleed or Double Block and Vent: \n The bleed or vent has been confirmed to be open visually, mechanically (rod), or by \nthe flow. \n Release from the bleed or vent will create no hazard. \n System pressure will not exceed the rating. Piping system rated by pressure and \ntemperature (most significant for steam). \n The bleed-off vent is large enough to release pressure. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 31 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 31 of 52 \n5.15.5 Conditions under which use of double block and bleed is not permitted: \n There has been a previous failure under the same or like conditions. \n System pressure is not known. \n Piping rating not known. \n The piping system is not rated. \n The system is susceptible to hammering or other uncontrolled pressure surges. \n There is no procedure for bringing a system up to pressure during startup. \n There have been changes that could affect the flow of material made to the system \nsince its last operation. \n5.15.6 Conditions for the use of a Single Valve: \n System pressure will not exceed 75% of the rating. Piping system rated by pressure \nand temperature (most significant for steam). \n No defects or problems are detected by careful inspection of the valve. \n Stored energy and energy potential are extremely low/minimal, and the maximum \nsystem pressure does not exceed the rating. Piping system rated by pressure and \ntemperature (Most significant for steam). \n The material being valved off creates no significant hazard. \n5.15.7 Conditions under which use of a Single Valve is not permitted: \n There has been a previous failure under the same or like conditions. \n System pressure is not known. \n Valve rating is not known, or the valve is not rated. \n The system is susceptible to hammering or other uncontrolled pressure surges. \n There is no procedure for bringing a system up to pressure during startup. \n Significant changes have been made to the system since its last operation. \n When the system contains acids, caustic or poisonous material of such strength or \nconcentration, that contact can cause injury. \n The Confined Space Standard requires blank, blind, spool, mis-align, plug or double \nblock, and bleed for isolation. \n5.15.8 Start-Up of Equipment – A thorough inspection must be conducted to ensure all \npersons exposed to stored energy safety hazards are informed that the equipment is \nto be started. \n5.15.9 Ensure all employees are removed from the exposure before starting the equipment. \n5.15.10 Specify any other necessary steps which must be taken before equipment is started. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 32 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 32 of 52 \n5.16 Periodic Inspections of energy control and Verification Procedures \nand Alternative Controls \n5.16.1 Periodic inspections of energy isolation and verification procedures and alternative \ncontrols shall be conducted at least once per year, and consist of the following \nrequired elements: \n5.16.2 Shall be performed by an authorized employee other than the ones utilizing the energy \ncontrol and verification procedure or alternative measure(s) being inspected. \n5.16.3 Shall be conducted to correct any deviations or inadequacies identified during the \ninspection. \n5.16.4 Shall include a review between the inspector and each authorized employee of that \nemployee's responsibilities under the inspected energy isolation and verification \nprocedure. \n5.16.5 The periodic inspection findings shall be documented using the periodic inspections: \nenergy isolation and verification procedure and alternative Control Form, CCS-HSS-\nFRM-0006.0.  \n5.16.6 All Operating Section and Shift Managers will be required to complete one periodic \ninspection of an alternative control per week where applicable. \n5.16.7 All Maintenance Section and Shift Managers must complete one periodic inspection of \nan energy isolation and verification procedure activity for every standard downturn and \noutage. \n5.16.8 All plant Safety Personnel will be required to complete one periodic inspection of \nalternative control or energy isolation and verification procedure activity per week. \n5.16.9 You may group distinct energy isolation and verification procedures associated with \nsimilar machines or equipment and consider the group of distinct energy isolation and \nverification procedures to be a single procedure for conducting a periodic inspection if \nthe machines or equipment in the group have the same or similar types of control \nmeasures.  \n5.16.10 You may elect to group energy isolation and verification procedures described above \nand then inspect a representative number of such employees implementing one \nprocedure within each group. \n5.16.11 This approach is acceptable if the inspection sampling reflects division servicing and \nmaintenance operations and hazardous energy control practices for the inspected \nenergy isolation and verification procedure. \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 33 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 33 of 52 \n \n \n \n \n5.16.12 The periodic inspections must contain at least two components:  \n An inspection of each energy isolation and verification procedure (or grouping), \nand  \n A review of each employee's responsibilities under the energy isolation and \nverification procedure being inspected.  \n5.16.13 Each energy control and verification procedure (or grouping) must be inspected \nannually to ensure that the lockout/tryout verification program is correctly utilized. \n5.16.14 These inspections must demonstrate the energy isolation and verification procedures \nand must be performed while authorized employees perform servicing and \nmaintenance activities on machines or equipment. \n5.16.15 The employee performing the periodic inspection does not have to observe every \nauthorized employee implementing the energy isolation and verification procedure on \nthe machine or equipment they are authorized to perform servicing and maintenance \nto meet the requirements. The inspector participating in the review when the lockout \nis used needs to: \n Observe a representative number of such employees while implementing the \nenergy isolation and verification procedure, and \n Talk with all other authorized employees even though they may not implement \nthe energy isolation and verification procedure. \n5.16.16 The periodic inspection is not required to be conducted in separate one-on-one \nmeetings; it can involve the inspector meeting simultaneously with a group of \nauthorized employees. Group meetings can be one of the most effective methods to \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 34 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 34 of 52 \nconduct the inspection and clarify to the employees that they must follow the energy \nisolation and verification procedure carefully. \n5.17 Operating, Repairing, Servicing, and Inspection of Equipment \n5.17.1 Only a trained operator or maintenance person may energize/de-energize and operate \na machine.  \n5.17.2 Do not operate, repair or test any machinery, electrical apparatus, or other equipment \nunless part of your assigned duties.  \n5.17.3 Do not lubricate, clean, repair, inspect or operate a machine you do not understand. \nWhen in doubt, ask your manager.  \n5.17.4 If you are authorized to operate machinery, follow all safety procedures, including the \nJSA, JSHA (Job Safety and Health Analysis), Department Rules, and Manufacturer’s \nrecommendations established for the machine or equipment. \n \n \n \n5.17.5 Authorized Cleveland-Cliffs Steel employees must perform the energy isolation and \nverification procedures including de-energizing, lockout, verification, and tryout before \nservicing and maintaining machines and equipment.  \n5.17.6 All employees shall be trained on the energy isolation and verification procedures to \nensure that each employee understands the purpose and function of this energy \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 35 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 35 of 52 \nisolation standard and the knowledge and skills required for safe application, usage, \nand removal of energy controls. \n5.17.7 Operators of machines, cranes, and other equipment must secure the equipment \nunder the established energy isolation and verification procedure or other procedure \nbefore leaving the job unless relieved on the job by another operator. \n5.17.8 Any unsafe machine to operate must be de-energized and locked out. Notify a \nmanager of any broken or unsafe machinery or equipment. \n5.17.9 When opening an electric safety or disconnect switch:  \n Do not open under load \n Stand to the side of the disconnect box, not in front of it \n Turn face away from switch (reduce risk of being blinded or damage to breathing)  \n Hold breath (so if arc happens, you do not inhale liquid metal) \n Close eyes (so if arc happens, you are not blinded) \n Throw switch \n5.17.10 Contact your manager if it is impossible to stand clear from the front of the box. \n5.17.11 Only electrically qualified and authorized personnel are permitted to open a \ndisconnect switch to verify that the knife blades are in the open position. \n5.17.12 Do not remove any personal safety lock unless you place it. Remove only your lock \nonly after you are sure all personnel is in the clear.  \n5.17.13 Do not remove a DANGER or CAUTION sign unless you placed it, and then only \nafter you are sure the dangerous condition has been corrected.  \n5.17.14 Contact your manager if a lock must be removed and the person who placed it \ncannot be found. The lock shall be removed by following Safety Lock Removal \nIncident Report Form (CCS-HSS-FRM-0007.0). \n \n \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 36 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 36 of 52 \n5.17.15 Only authorized personnel can adjust machinery in motion with a written procedure \napproved by the plant General Manager and Health, Safety, and Security Manager. \n If you are authorized to make minor production adjustments on machines, follow the \nprocedures that have been approved. \n5.17.16 Be sure to guard against the movement of parts or machinery which might injure you \nor others. Energy control and verification procedures must be followed during the \nservice or maintenance of the equipment. \n5.17.17 Machinery must not be operated if guards have been removed for repairs or \nadjustments. \n5.17.18 Do not remove locks or energize equipment until all guards and safety devices are \ncorrectly replaced, secured, and operable. \n5.17.19 Machine guarding and safety devices shall be affixed to the machine or secured \nelsewhere as designed by the equipment manufacturer and approved by completing \nan Equipment Safety Inspection and Startup Procedure Form. \n5.17.20 Pressure must be bled off before repair or service work on hydraulic, steam, gas, air, \noil, water lines, or connected equipment.  \n Flow valves must be closed and locked, and pressure relief valves must be locked in \nthe open position.  \n Accumulators must be discharged. \n5.17.21 Valve lockouts must be made following energy control and verification procedures \nbefore any repair or service. \n5.17.22 Before the flow of steam, gas, air, oil, or water is restored to a system, the person \ndoing so must ensure that no one can be injured as the systems are activated. \n5.17.23 Use extreme caution when working around conveyor systems, especially conveyor \nbelts.  \n5.17.24 Do not shovel onto moving belts unless authorized by specific written procedures \nand all guarding is in place.  \n5.17.25 Make sure the emergency stop is functioning correctly. \n5.17.26 Moving rolls shall not be touched by hand at any time.  \n5.17.27 Work rolls shall not be cleaned or polished by hand in the mill and under power. \n5.17.28 If work rolls are installed in the mill and must be cleaned or polished by hand, they \nshall be locked out. \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 37 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 37 of 52 \n5.17.29 Work rolls installed in the mill and moving or under power may be cleaned or \npolished under the following conditions: \n Tools shall be provided to facilitate the cleaning or polishing of work rolls with the \nhands at a safe distance from the moving rolls.  \n A procedure directs using a tool(s) to keep hands away from the moving rolls.  \n The cleaning or polishing of the work rolls shall be performed at the side of the mill \nopposite the work roll's in-running nip point. If necessary, the direction of rotation of \nthe work rolls may be reversed to eliminate the in-running nip-point hazard. \n5.17.30 The strip shall not be touched with any body part when moving under tension. \n The strip may be positioned or worked on using extension/hands-free tools when the \nline is stopped or in jog mode. \n Extension/hands-free tools shall be used when clearing jambs and removing scrap \nfrom the line unless the scrap has been cut into easily manageable, i.e., sample \nwidth or smaller, pieces. \n A variance to this rule may be granted by the Plant General Manager and the Health, \nSafety, and Security Manager after all alternatives have been exhausted. In addition, \na written safe procedure for performing the job must be developed. \n5.18   LOTO for Industrial Vehicles \n5.18.1 Any vehicle has the potential to contain various types of hazardous energy: \n Mechanical hazards associated with start-up. \n Mechanical motions due to moving power transmission components. \n Mechanical hazards with disc brake springs and tire components. \n Hydraulic hazards associated with fluid pressure and loss. \n Gravitational energy from elevated vehicles and lifts. \n Gravitational energy from elevated truck-mounted equipment such as dump beds. \n Airbag explosion. \n Electric battery shock, arc, or burns. \n Fuel and fluid system fire or explosion. \n Hot or cryogenic fluid and surface hazards. \n Chemical energy (e.g., battery acid, coolants, lubricants, etc.). \n5.18.2 Before servicing an industrial vehicle, an initial step is to remove the ignition key from \nthe ignition switch.  \n The repairperson shall maintain sole possession of that key, placing it in their pocket \nor a group lockout box if more than one person is servicing the vehicle. \n Doors to the vehicle should also be locked. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 38 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 38 of 52 \n5.18.3 If the above two steps are insufficient to prevent an engine from accidentally starting, \nthe repair person shall disconnect the battery cables. \n Disconnecting batteries is essential when working on or around cooling fans, which \ncan automatically start up after removing the ignition key. \n It is good to allow an engine to cool down sufficiently. Doing so will allow heat and \nsubsequent pressure to dissipate, helping control those forms of hazardous energy. \n5.18.4 Depending on the type of vehicle being worked on and the service being performed, \nthe repair person must either lower or block-up components held up by hydraulic \npressure, such as dump beds and aerial lifts. It may also be necessary to chock or \nblock wheels to prevent a vehicle from rolling. \n5.18.5 Dump beds can be incredibly perplexing. Manufacturer-provided props often do not \nprovide the clearance necessary to perform many under-bed repairs. This incites some \nrepair persons to create their support braces, many of which do not provide adequate \nprotection.  \n The equipment manufacturer shall be contacted to discuss an alternative body prop \nor another suitable method to perform the work. \n Remember that reenergizing a vehicle or component is often necessary for testing or \ntroubleshooting.  \n A procedure that defines the sequence of actions necessary to accomplish the task \nsafely shall be developed. \n5.18.6 It is imperative to check its service guidelines with the specific industrial vehicle \nmanufacturer. Those service guidelines can double as guidelines to help develop \nenergy isolation and verification procedures. \n5.18.7 Thorough energy isolation and verification procedures must be developed for shop \nequipment, including lifts, jacks, tire changers, wheel balancers, and pneumatic \n(compressed air) systems.  \n Procedures should be aligned with the equipment manufacturers’ service guidelines. \n \n It is essential that the industrial vehicle energy isolation and training program helps to \nensure that employees understand all relevant energy sources, methods of isolating \nor controlling those energy sources, and methods to verify effective energy control. \nTherefore, employee retraining may be necessary from time to time.  \n \n \n \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 39 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 39 of 52 \n5.19  Tagout Alternative/ Tagout System Limitations \n5.19.1 A tag must only be used instead of an equipment lock when there is no means to apply \na lock. \n5.19.2 If a machine cannot be locked out, it must be tagged. If energy isolating devices \ncannot accommodate locks, a tag shall be used to warn employees that a machine is \nde-energized for servicing. \n5.19.3 The procedure for installing and removing tags is the same as the lockout procedure. \n5.19.4 Tags are only a warning device affixed to energy isolating devices and do not provide \nthe physical restraint on those devices provided by a lock. \n5.19.5 When a tag is attached to an energy isolating means, it is not to be removed without \nauthorization from the authorized person responsible. It is also never to be bypassed, \nignored, or otherwise defeated. \n5.19.6 Tags must be legible and understandable by all authorized employees, affected \nemployees, and all other employees whose work operations are or may be in the area \nto be effective. \n5.19.7 Tags and their means of attachment must be made of materials that will withstand the \nenvironmental conditions encountered in the workplace. \n5.19.8 Employees shall be made to understand that tags may evoke a false sense of security \nand their meaning need to be understood as part of the overall energy control \nprogram. \n5.19.9 Tags must be securely fastened to the energy isolating devices so they cannot be \ninadvertently or accidentally detached during use. \n5.19.10 Tags shall include the employee's name, division, and date. \n5.19.11 Like a lock, the tag must be securely fastened, in a position to be seen by all \nemployees, and standardized. Therefore, the following safety tags (Figure 9) have \nbeen standardized, and no others are permitted to be used: \n \nFigure 9 (Above) - Approved Durable Tag with Grommet \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 40 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 40 of 52 \n5.20 Alternative Control - Requirements \n5.20.1 You cannot use control circuit type devices such as start/stop push buttons, e-stops, \nselector switches, presence sensing devices, or limiting switches instead of energy-\nisolating devices for equipment lockout. This is because these control circuit type \ndevices do not physically isolate equipment from the equipment’s energy source. As a \nresult, unanticipated circumstances such as welded contacts or a loose wire shorting \nout can cause unexpected energization or startup.  \n5.20.2 Under the “minor servicing,” circuitry meeting the control reliability requirements of \nANSI Z244.1-2016 provides the alternative control measures required to control \nhazardous energy. \n Control reliability is the capability of the control system and related interfacing to \nachieve a safe state in the event of a failure. \n \n For example, an interlock or e-stop having a dual-channel (redundant) circuit with \na self-monitoring safety module typically meets the control reliability \nrequirements. \n A basic start/stop circuit is equipped with a single-contact button (single channel), \neven though it may be “safety-rated” (positive contacts). \n Due to the number of times the e-stop is cycled and the simplicity of the circuit, \nthere are still several chances for a single component to fail, leading to circuit \nfailure.  \n Because of the lack of redundancy and self-checking circuitry, this single-channel \ne-stop circuit is not considered control reliable.  \n \n If you are considering incorporating control reliable safety control circuits, a thorough \npracticability/justification analysis (CCS-HSS-FRM-0009.0), risk assessment (CCS-\nHSS-FRM-0010.0), and other alternative control evaluations (see Appendix A) must \nbe done to document and verify the necessary integrity and performance levels of the \ncontrol system. This ensures you are providing equivalent and effective employee \nprotection. \n \n The machine supplier, competent machine integrator, or internal engineering delegate \ncan only make the above control reliable safety control circuits determination. These \npersons can determine a machine’s performance level according to (EN) ISO \nstandards which correspond to the machine performance level expectations. \nMachines with interlocks, e-stops, or presence sensing technologies have an up-to-\ndate (no significant changes made to machine or wiring) machine performance level \nreview on file.  \n5.20.3 The exemption for minor tool changes and adjustments and other minor servicing \nactivities is a unique situation and shall not be used to avoid lockout/tryout. \n5.20.4 Lockout/tryout shall be used unless the division manager can demonstrate to the \nHealth, Safety, and Security Manager that an alternative control provides equal or \ngreater protection. When energy isolation is not used, approved alternative control \nshall be used. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 41 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 41 of 52 \n5.20.5 There are three specific criteria to determine if the minor servicing exception would \napply to a particular activity: \n The activity must be conducted during normal production operations. \n The activity must be: \n \n Routine: The activity must be a regular course of procedure and follow established \npractices. \n Repetitive: The activity must be regularly repeated as part of the production \nprocess. \n Integral: The activity must be essential to the production process. \n5.20.6 Before alternative controls are used, the following shall be completed and approved \nper ANSI Z244.1-2016, The Control of Hazardous Energy Lockout and Alternative \nControls: \n A practicability/justification analysis per clause 8.2.1 (CCS-HSS-FRM-0009.0). \n A risk assessment as per clause 8.2.2 (CCS-HSS-FRM-0010.0).  \n Other alternative control evaluations as described in clauses 8.2.3 – 8.2.14 (See \nAppendix A). \n5.20.7 Alternative controls shall be added to the safe job procedure (JSHA or JSA) if \napproved. \n5.20.8 There shall be a master list in each division of these approved alternative controls. \n5.20.9 Alternative controls must be under the exclusive control of the employee performing \nthe minor servicing. \n5.20.10 These alternative controls must enable the employee to safely perform the servicing \ntask without being exposed to the energization or activation of the equipment or the \nrelease of stored energy. \n5.20.11 If the minor servicing exception criteria are not met in full, this Standard is applicable, \nand the equipment or machinery must be de-energized. In addition, all potentially \nhazardous energy must be brought to a zero-energy state. \n5.21 Contract Workers (Contractors, Sub-Contractors, Service Providers) \n5.21.1 When contractors work at a Cleveland-Cliffs Steel facility, they must follow their written \nlockout/tryout standard and adhere to the Cleveland-Cliffs Steel Energy Isolation \nStandard.  \n5.21.2 All contract workers must comply with these requirements, beginning with, but not \nlimited to, having a red personal safety lock and lock identification. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 42 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 42 of 52 \n5.21.3 Contract workers shall identify personal safety locks with employee and contractor \ncompany names. \n Contract workers are permitted to use a durable tag with a grommet attached to the \nshank of their personal safety lock due to outages and utilization of travelers. \n5.21.4 Contract workers are not permitted to operate any energy isolation devices. Cleveland-\nCliffs Steel employees shall perform this task. A variance to this rule may be granted \nby the Plant General Manager and Health, Safety, and Security Manager after written \nsafe procedure for performing the job are submitted and approved. \n5.21.5 This Energy Isolation Safety Standard will be distributed to our current contract \nworkforce base through ISNetworld. However, each steel plant’s Health, Safety, and \nSecurity Department will be required to conduct or administer a face-to-face meeting \non the subject with each contract company's representatives to: \n Verify each contract company working at your facility has received a copy of the \nStandard. \n Answer or record (to later answer) any comments, questions, or concerns. \n Ensure (as much as possible) that each contract company is or will be conducting \ntraining on this Standard with their contract employees. \n5.21.6 Each facility’s employee/management committee will determine how to address \ntraining on this Standard for vendors, visitors, and contract workers not associated with \nISNetworld. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 43 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor clusters,\" or plain \"grounds.\" Personal \nprotective grounds are used whenever workers perform tasks on electrical power \nsystems that may become reenergized for some reason by the reclosing of switches or \ncircuit breakers, static voltages, and induced voltages in outdoor substations or lines \nand capacitive discharges. \n6.9.3 The use of personal protective grounding is covered by OSHA If components are used \nthat are less than well-tried or less than well-tried designs are implemented, the \narchitecture/structure can significantly improve the system’s reliability. \n6.12.3 Well-Tried Components \n A well-tried component is a component that is either widely used in the past with \nsuccessful results in similar applications or made and verified by the component \nmanufacturer using principles that demonstrate its suitability and reliability for safety-\nrelated applications. \n The components used can be checked and verified on new machinery and \nequipment and existing equipment used in the field. Thus, both users and suppliers \nof machinery can verify the design of existing machinery in the field. \n6.12.4 Well-Tried Designs \n A well-tried design is a system that has been demonstrated, based on an analysis of \noperational experience, for a specific configuration of an element to show that the \nlikelihood of dangerous systematic faults is acceptably low. \n6.12.5 Common Cause Failure (CCF) \n Common cause failures refer to a failure resulting from one or more events, causing \nconcurrent failures of two or more separate elements in a redundant \narchitecture/structure subsystem, leading to a dangerous failure. \n A CCF usually results in two or more failures from a single event or cause. \nTherefore, the design should be assessed to establish the effectiveness of the \nmeasures used to safeguard against CCF. \n6.12.6 Fault Tolerance \n Fault tolerance pertains to the ability of an alternative control to achieve a safe state \nin the event of a fault. Faults can include leaking valves, electrical shorts, contactors \nwelding shut, mechanical fractures/breakage, overheating components, and \nenvironmental exposure. \n Fault tolerance is determined by the circuit’s architecture and is defined as \ncategories and performance levels in ISO 13849-1 or “Control Reliability” as defined \nin ANSI B11.19. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 47 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 47 of 52 \n6.12.7 Exclusivity or Individual Control \n Examples include, but are not limited to: \n Locks \n Size of working zone \n Proximity to control stations, pendants, or devices \n Locking devices \n Passwords \n Enabling devices \n Hold to run controls \n Trapped key systems \n Presence sensing devices (under some conditions) \n Interlock blocking device \n RFID systems \n Factors that reduce exclusivity shall be considered in alternate risk reduction \nmeasures \n Examples include, but are not limited to: \n Key reproducibility \n Manual manipulation of controls or devices \n Password protection and management \n Visibility of the hazardous area from the reset location (line of sight) \n6.12.8 Tamper Resistance \n Whereas exclusivity pertains to a person’s ability to control the release of energy, \ntamper resistance refers to the ability of a person to tamper intentionally, resulting in \na system inadvertently energizing or releasing energy. A lock exhibits good tamper \nresistance due to the physical properties of the lock. Tagout has less tamper \nresistance as defeating a plastic zip tie requires less force and more easily obtained \ntools (for example, pliers versus bolt cutters). A warning or tag exhibits even less \ntamper resistance. \n Selection of system components may also serve to resist tampering (bypass, \njumpering, defeat of interlocks, etc.). \n The physical layout of the equipment may achieve tamper resistance. For example, \nrestricting access to areas is another form of physical layout that improves tamper \nresistance. \n The physical configuration of components may also achieve tamper resistance. \nDesigns that limit or prohibit mechanical manipulation of the controls improve tamper \nresistance. \n Tamper resistance need not be physical. For example, passwords that restrict \naccess represent another form of tamper resistance. \n Another form of tamper resistance is technical sophistication or complexity. Tamper \nresistance is improved if bypassing a system requires having access to, and being \nable to read, the system schematics. Systems requiring specialized skills to operate \nhave greater tamper resistance than those without. \n \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 48 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 48 of 52 \n6.12.9 Program to Support \n The documentation of the tasks which may be performed using a specific alternative \ncontrol is intended to prevent task creep. \n6.12.10 Procedures in Place \n The procedures in place to support an alternative control shall be evaluated. \nAlternative controls shall include developing, documenting, and using \nprocedures when affected persons are engaged in activities requiring the \ncontrol of hazardous energy. \n6.12.11 Periodic Check and Test \n Where alternative controls are used to control hazardous energy, the need for \nchecking is especially important because, over time, equipment, operations, and \nwork methods can change in small and large ways. Without a program that requires \nperiodic checking and testing, alternative controls can become outdated to the point \nthat they no longer provide adequate protection. \n Checking and testing shall also include correcting any deviations or inadequacies \nidentified. \n6.12.12 Review by a Qualified Person \n The persons developing the alternative control should use due diligence throughout \nthe process to achieve high confidence in the results. Confidence can be improved \nby consulting others with the appropriate knowledge and expertise and having other \nqualified persons review the design. \n \n  \n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 49 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 49 of 52 \n6.13 Appendix B – Lockout/Tryout Implementation Flow Diagram \n \n \n  \n\n\n--- SOURCE PDF PAGE 50 ---\nEnergy Isolation Safety Standard   \n \nFor reference only.   Invalid if printed.  \nPage 50 of 52 \n6.14 Appendix C – Functional Lockout Flowchart \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n  \nYes \nIs system or  \nequipment in \noperation? \nNo \nIsolate all energy sources \nTurn off all operating controls \nIdentify the type(s) and magnitude of energy \nNotify all affected employees \nFollow normal cycle stop \nor shut down process \nEquipment or process needs \nmaintenance or service where a \nlockout is required \nNo Is system \nproperly locked \nout & tried \nout? Page 2 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 1 Purpose/Scope 1.1 Establish minimum standards for all Cleveland-Cliffs Steel Operations to protect employees and contracted workers that are exposed to potential falls greater than 4 feet or working above dangerous equipment. In addition, it establishes the scope, training, and use of fall protection systems by Cleveland-Cliffs Steel employees in Cleveland-Cliffs Steel facilities. 1.2 This standard shall establish a means to analyze elevated work tasks, determine appropriate controls and protect personnel from fall to below hazards. All people performing work on Cleveland-Cliffs Steel property shall follow and comply with the requirements in this standard, as it has been developed to meet or exceed applicable OSHA regulatory requirements. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 3 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 2 Responsibilities and Authorities 2.1 This Standard was jointly developed with participation from the represented workforce and management. 2.2 Cleveland-Cliffs Employees and Contractor Employees shall: • Notify supervisors or other management personnel when fall exposures exist and are not adequately controlled. • Attend required training. • Properly wear all required fall protection equipment when performing a task or activity in an unprotected area where a fall potential of greater than 4 feet exist. • Inspect fall protection equipment before use to identify any deficiencies. • Maintain and store personal fall protection equipment following this standard. • Make personal fall protection components available for annual inspection. 2.3 Supervisors and Management Personnel shall in addition to the above: • Assess the work area for potential fall hazards, working-at-height scenarios and use the hierarchy of controls to mitigate fall exposures. • Ensure that only trained and authorized personnel are assigned work that requires fall protection equipment. • Ensure employees attend required fall protection training. • Ensure training is documented and records are available. • Ensure that approved fall protection equipment is available in adequate supply. 2.4 Engineering Department shall: • Ensure a documented process is in place for engineering, inspecting, and testing of fall protection systems. • Confirm the qualification and certification of 3rd party inspectors of engineered systems. • Verify and document structural integrity of roofs and other structures for fall-through protection. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 4 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard • Maintain documents pertaining to fall protection system design as required for regularly scheduled system recertification. 2.5 Purchasing shall: • Ensure only approved fall protection equipment is made available that is compliant with regulatory requirements and Cleveland-Cliffs standards. • Ensure contractors that have the potential for working at heights, understand the requirements stipulated in this Standard, and that contractor personnel have the necessary training to use and wear fall protection equipment. 2.6 Site Safety Department shall: • Establish and maintain guidance and documents in compliance with this Standard. • Review and approve the need for new and additional fall protection equipment, including Engineered Systems and components (Gemtor, Railmaster, etc.) before use. • Audit for compliance with this Standard. • Ensure Fall Protection Training is made available and is conducted in compliance with this Standard. • Conduct investigation for all fall to below incidents. • Evaluate employee needs for special harnesses or other alternative fall protection needs. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 6 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 3.2 Definitions Anchorage or Anchorage Point: A secure point of attachment for lifelines, lanyards, or deceleration devices capable of supporting 5,000 pounds per person or is designed by a qualified person and are a part of a complete fall protection system maintaining a safety factor of at least two. Arresting Force: The force generated by arresting the test weight that is transmitted through the fall arresting system components to the anchorage or load cell. Authorized Person: A person approved or assigned to perform a specific type of duty or duties or to be at a specific location or job site (e.g., building maintenance, roof repair, etc.). Employees who are required to wear fall protection during work activities. Body Harness: A design of straps which may be secured about the employee in a manner that will distribute the fall arrest forces over at least the thighs, pelvis, waist, chest, and shoulders with means for attaching it to other components of a personal fall arrest system. Competent Person: A person who is capable of identifying existing and predictable hazards in any personal fall protection system or any component of it, as well as in their application and uses with related equipment, and who has authorization to take prompt, corrective action to eliminate the identified hazards. Cage/Basket Guard: An enclosure that is fastened to the side rails of the fixed ladder or to the structure to encircle the climbing space of the ladder for the safety of the person who must climb the ladder. Connector: A device used to couple (connect) parts of the personal fall arrest system together. Carabiner: A connector component comprised of a trapezoidal or oval-shaped body with a normally closed gate. The gate, when released, automatically closes to retain the object. All carabiners used in fall protection systems must be the automatic locking type and have a minimum tensile strength of 5,000 pounds (22 kN). Dangerous Equipment: Equipment (such as pickling or galvanizing tanks, degreasing units, machinery, electrical equipment, and other units) which, as a result of form or function, may be hazardous to employees who fall onto or into such equipment. Deceleration Device: Any mechanism which serves to lessen the arresting force on the body during a fall. Deceleration Distance: The vertical distance a falling employee travels from the point at which the deceleration device begins to operate, excluding lifeline elongation and free fall distance, until stopping. It is measured as the distance between the location of an employee's body harness attachment point at the moment of activation (at the onset of fall arrest forces) of the deceleration device during a fall, and the location of that attachment point after the employee comes to a full stop. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 7 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard Fall Arrest System: A system that prevents a worker from falling and contacting a lower level. It consists of an anchorage, connectors, a full body harness, and may include a lanyard, deceleration device, lifeline, or any combination of the before-mentioned items. Fall Clearance Calculation: The total calculated distance required to prevent workers from hitting the ground or lower level, in case of a fall. Free Fall: The act of falling before a personal fall arrest system begins to apply force to arrest the fall. Free Fall Distance: The vertical displacement of the fall arrest attachment point on the employee's body harness between the onset of the fall and just before the system begins to apply force to arrest the fall. This distance excludes deceleration distance, and lifeline/lanyard elongation, but includes any deceleration device slide distance or self-retracting lifeline/lanyard extension before they operate and fall arrest forces occur. (Always minimize Free Fall Distance not to exceed 6 feet.) Fixed Ladder: A ladder which is permanently attached to a structure, building or equipment. (Does not include ship stairs, step bolts or manhole steps.) All new or modified fixed ladders greater than 24 feet shall have a ladder safety system or fall arrest system. Existing fixed ladders must have a ladder safety system or fall arrest system by November 2036. Guardrail System: A barrier erected along an unprotected or exposed side, edge, or other areas of a walking/working surface to prevent workers from falling to a lower level. The system consists of a top rail at 42 inches (plus or minus three inches) above the walking surface, a midrail at a height midway between the top edge of the guardrail and the walking surface. A toe board is required to ensure falling object protection along the exposed edge of overhead walking/working surfaces sufficient to protect employees below. Handrail System: A barrier erected to prevent employees from falling to lower levels. Handrails are not less than 30 inches and not more than 38 inches measured from the edge of the stair tread. There must be a minimum of 2.5 inches clearance between the handrail and any other object. Lanyard: A flexible line of rope, cable, or strap with self-locking snap hook connectors at each end for connecting to body harnesses or deceleration devices. Ladder Safety System: A system (usually consisting of a carrier, safety sleeve, lanyard, connectors, and body harness) attached to a fixed ladder designed to eliminate or reduce the possibility of a worker falling from the ladder. Leading Edge: The edge of a floor, roof or other walking/working surface which changes location as additional floor, roof, etc., is placed or constructed. A leading edge is considered an unprotected side or edge. Lifeline: A component consisting of a flexible line for connection to an anchorage at one end to hang vertically (vertical lifelines), or for connection to anchorages at both ends to stretch horizontally (horizontal lifeline), and which serves as a means for connecting other components of a personal fall arrest system to the anchorage. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 8 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard Low-Slope Roof: A roof having a slope less than or equal to 4 in 12 (vertical to horizontal). Personal Fall Arrest System: System used to arrest an employee in a fall from a working level. It consists of an anchorage, connectors, full body harness and a self-retractable lifeline/lanyard. Personal Fall Protection: A system that is used to arrest an employee in a fall from a working level. The fall protection must consist of a full body harness, a lanyard with a deceleration device and an anchorage or lifeline with approved hooking devices as specified in Cleveland- Cliffs Fall Protection Standard. Qualified Person: One who, by possession of a recognized degree, certificate, or professional standing, or who by extensive knowledge, training and experience, has successfully demonstrated ability to solve or resolve problems relating to the subject matter, the work or the project. Rope Grab: A cam-type deceleration device that travels on a lifeline and automatically engages the lifeline and locks to arrest an employee’s fall. Safety Factor: An added margin of security against risk. Safety Net Systems: A horizontal or semi-horizontal, cantilever-style barrier that uses a netting system to stop falling workers before they contact a lower level or obstruction. Safety Monitor: A competent person responsible for recognizing and warning workers of fall hazards. Self-Retracting Lifeline/Lanyard: Deceleration device containing a drum-wound line which can be slowly extracted from, or retracted onto, the drum under slight tension during normal employee movement, and which after the onset of a fall, automatically locks the drum and arrests the fall. Steep Roof: Roof having slope of greater than 4 in 12 (vertical to horizontal). Snap Hook: A connector comprised of a hook-shaped member with a closed keeper, which may be opened to permit the hook to receive an object, and when released, automatically closes to retain the object. Snap hooks must be self-closing with a self-locking keeper, which remains closed and locked until unlocked and pressed open for connection or disconnection, thus preventing the opportunity for the object to “roll out” of the snap hook. Snap hooks used for fall protection systems must have a 3,600-pound gate strength. Swing Fall: The potential for a worker using a personal fall arrest system to fall and swing in a pendulum motion, subjecting the worker to strike a fixed object or other hazards. This can occur if a worker falls and the anchorage is not directly overhead, and a fall allows the worker to swing toward the anchor point. Tie Off: The act of a person wearing personal fall protection equipment, connecting directly to an anchorage point. 100 % tie off is required when exposed to a fall hazard while using Travel Restraint or Fall Arrest systems. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 9 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard Toe Board: A solid horizontal guard installed along the exposed edge of overhead walking/working surfaces to prevent the fall of materials and equipment to lower levels and protect employees below. They must be a minimum of 3.5 inches and not have an opening more than 0.25 inches above the walking/working surface. Total Fall Distance: Adding the free fall distance, lanyard deceleration distance depending on the equipment (24” for ANSI Z359.14-2014 Class A, 54” for ANSI Z359.14-2014 Class B, 42” for ANSI Z359.14-2021 Class 1 and Class 2), harness stretch, the distance your body extended from the dorsal ring of your harness attached to your anchorage point, and a 3-foot safety factor. Trauma Straps: A pair of straps attached to a full body harness that allows a worker, who is suspended, to stand up in their harness and to relieve the pressure being applied to the arteries and veins around the top of the legs. Travel Restraint (Tether) Line: A rope or wire rope used to transfer forces from a body support to an anchorage or anchorage connector in a travel restraint system. Travel Restraint System: A combination of an anchorage, anchorage connector, lanyard (or other means of connection) and body support intended to be used by an employee to limit travel to prevent exposure to a fall hazard. A travel restraint system is used such that it does not support any portion of the employee's weight; otherwise, the system would be a positioning system or a personal fall arrest system. Travel restraint lines must be capable of sustaining a tensile load of at least 5,000 pounds (22.2 kN). Unprotected Sides or Edges: Any side or edge (except at entrances to points of access) of a walking/working surface; e.g., floor, roof, ramp, or runway where there is no wall or guardrail system at least 39 inches high. Walking/Working Surface: Any surface, whether horizontal or vertical, on which an employee walks or works, including but not limited to floors, roofs, ramps, bridges, runways, framework, and concrete reinforcing steel. Work Positioning: A system consisting of a body harness, lanyards, and anchorage devices rigged to allow a person to be supported on an elevated vertical surface, such as a wall or fixed ladder, and work with both hands free. Attachments should be at the workers hips as preferred method while using a work positioning system. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 10 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 4 Resources 4.1 Training is required for authorized persons and contractors who work in areas where fall hazards exist and fall protection equipment is required. 4.2 Fall prevention, travel limiting and fall arrest equipment must be purchased and provided to employees who are exposed to falls greater than 4 feet or working above dangerous equipment. 4.3 Fall protection equipment will be inspected as appropriate. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 11 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 5 Requirements 5.1 Application 5.1.1 Cleveland-Cliffs Steel employees, contractors, sub-contractors, service providers, vendors and visitors when working at any elevation greater than four (4) feet from the floor, deck, platform, or ground level that is not adequately protected on all sides by standard handrails or guardrails shall be required to use personal fall protection. 5.1.2 Cleveland-Cliffs Steel employees, contractors, sub-contractors, service providers, vendors, and visitors when at any elevation above dangerous equipment shall be protected from falling into or onto the dangerous equipment by guardrails, equipment guards or appropriate fall protection systems. 5.1.3 Cleveland-Cliffs Steel employees, contractors, sub-contractors, service providers, vendors and visitors working in an aerial lift, scissors lift, on scaffolding without standard handrail or exposed to a fall hazard around an excavation (4 feet or deeper) are also required to use personal fall protection. 5.1.4 Every effort must be made by all workers to eliminate falls to a lower level or into or onto dangerous equipment. 5.1.5 Use the Take 5 process to assess the job and use the Hierarchy of Controls when controlling fall to below hazards. Examples: • Eliminate the need for elevated work by moving valves to floor level. • Guard from falls by installing permanent standard railing. • Use scaffolding with handrails, mid-rails, and toe boards. • Use aerial lifts. • Use travel restraint when other exposure-based controls are not available. • Use fall arrest systems when the work doesn’t allow for higher-level controls. • Safety Net System as the last line of protection when no other can be achieved. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 12 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 5.1.6 Distance alone from a fall to below hazard does not afford adequate protection. 5.1.7 All roofs at Cleveland-Cliffs Steel require fall off and fall through protection. Refer to the plant’s specific roof access procedure. Fall off protection must be used anytime an employee is not protected by standard walkway and handrails or other engineered system. Barricade and restrict access to roofs or other working surfaces where structural integrity concerns exist or where known fall hazards exist. 5.1.8 Travel Restraint devices are used to prevent employees from approaching an area where he or she may fall, such as in aerial and scissors lifts and other personal lifting devices. Only full-body harnesses are permissible as Travel Restraint devices. Lanyards used with the harness must have double locking snap hooks or double locking carabineers. 5.1.9 Travel Restraint systems shall require: • Full body harness equipped with trauma straps. • Non-shock absorbing lanyard. • Lanyard must be of a length that will not allow access to the fall hazard. • Anchorage capable of supporting at least 5,000 pounds per user or designed by a qualified person and are a part of a complete fall protection system maintaining a safety factor of at least two. • Any required welding on anchorage must be conducted by a person certified for the specific application and engineering specifications. The person must be certified for the welds required. Contact plant engineering for welding or attachment requirements prior to installation. 5.1.10 Personal fall arrest systems shall require: ABC A. Anchorage Point/Anchorage Connector • Anchorage capable of supporting at least 5,000 pounds per user or designed by a qualified person and are a part of a complete fall protection system maintaining a safety factor of at least two. • Any required welding on anchorage must be conducted by a person certified for the specific application and engineering specifications. The person must be certified for the welds required. Contact plant engineering for welding or attachment requirements prior to installation. • Anchorage is to be installed directly overhead when possible. • Workers should not walk more than six (6) feet away from anchorage when possible. B. Full body harness equipped with trauma straps. C. Connecting Device. Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 13 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard • Self-retracting lanyard (SRL). • When leading edge or sharp edge potential exists that can damage lanyards during a fall a Leading Edge SRL meeting the requirements of ANSI Z359.14 will be required. • Connection directly to the D-ring in the middle of the back, unless the manufacturer provides an approved connector (e.g., Dual Leg Personal SRL). • No additional lanyard or other lengthening devices (Short D-ring extenders are allowed if included in the design and any calculations where appropriate). • Any exception must be reviewed and approved in writing by the Site Safety Manager. Site Safety Department will review and approve all new and additional fall protection equipment, including Engineered Systems and components (Gemtor, Railmaster, etc.) prior to use. 5.1.11 Self-retracting lifelines/lanyard must be capable of sustaining a minimum tensile load of 5,000 pounds (22.2 kN) applied to the device with the lifeline or lanyard in the fully extended position. 5.1.12 D-rings and snap-hooks must be capable of sustaining a minimum tensile load of 5,000 pounds (22.2 kN). 5.1.13 All self-retractable lifeline/lanyard and connecting means shall have double locking snap hooks. 5.1.14 All lanyard, deceleration device, and full body harness connecting means shall be made of drop forged or pressed steel and cadmium plated, or other ANSI approved and tested materials. The surfaces shall be smooth and free of sharp edges. Note: Identify and document all electrical fall hazards. If an arc-rated harness is used when working near energized electrical sources, refer to the arc-rated harness manufacturers’ guidelines regarding recommended lanyard and connecting means. 5.1.15 All self-retractable lifeline/lanyard, full body harnesses and associated hardware shall be inspected before and after each use. 5.1.16 The anchorage (tie-off point) shall be capable of supporting at least 5,000 pounds per employee attached. If there is any doubt about the strength of the anchorage point, DO NOT TIE OFF; find an alternate anchorage point. 5.1.17 Employee and tools over 310 pounds: Ensure the combined weight (tools and body) does not exceed the limits of the equipment. If the system is used by an employee having a combined weight (tools and body weight) of 310 pounds or more, special personal fall arrest equipment will need to be ordered to accommodate the individual. Please contact the Safety Department for proper equipment, training and job set up if a special harness is needed. ∆ ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 14 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 5.1.18 If any part of a fall protection system is subjected to a free fall, all components shall be taken out of service immediately and given to the Safety Department for discussion with competent persons and disposition. 5.1.19 If any employee experiences a free fall, the Safety Department shall be notified immediately. 5.2 Engineered Systems 5.2.1 Lifeline systems must be engineered to have appropriate anchorages, the strength of line designed to hold a specific number of individuals connected to it, line strength to aid in the arrest of a fall, and durability to hold a fallen employee(s) suspended until a rescue can occur. Only a Qualified Person may design a fall protection system or certify an existing system or anchorage point. 5.2.2 Engineered Systems and components (Gemtor, Railmaster, etc.) must be reviewed by a competent person prior to the Safety Department’s approval to purchase and/or use. A competent person reviews the installation as a part of the fall protection system. 5.3 Tie off Procedures 5.3.1 When using a full-body harness for fall protection, connecting devices shall be attached to the D-ring in the middle of the back, unless the manufacturer provides an approved connector (e.g., Dual Leg Personal SRL). 5.3.2 Snap hooks, including pelican hooks and carabiners, shall be of a design, size, and be compatible with the member that they are attached in order to prevent unintentional disengagement by depression of the keeper or side loading the keeper beyond its rating. 5.3.3 Side D-rings are for travel restraint or work positioning only and shall never be used for fall arrest connections. Whenever possible, tie off to an anchorage point at or above the D-ring of the harness. 5.3.4 For fall arrest systems on ladder safety systems, a front D-ring will be used or a back D- ring will be used based on the system design. 5.3.5 All new or modified fixed ladders that extend more than 24 feet shall have a ladder safety system or fall arrest system. Existing fixed ladders must have ladder safety system or fall arrest system by November 2036. 5.3.6 Workers shall tie off in a manner that ensures no lower level is struck during a free fall. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 15 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 5.3.7 Completely understand Fall Clearance Distance. This is the total distance for the fall arrest equipment to stop a fall plus a 3-foot safety factor. Always consider the following when determining fall protection equipment and proper tie-off location: • Location and height of the anchorage. • Free Fall Distance. • The maximum arrest or deceleration distance, depending on the equipment (24” for ANSI Z359.14-2014 Class A, 54” for ANSI Z359.14-2014 Class B, 42” for ANSI Z359.14-2021 Class 1 and Class 2). Class 1 must be attached to an anchor at or above the D-ring. • The distance that any deployable device will expand. • Stretch of harness and other components. • The height of the employee. • Swing Fall or deflection of non-rigid anchors such as horizontal lifelines. • Safety factor of 3 feet. • Workers should not walk more than six (6) feet away from anchorage, when possible, to minimize free fall distance and swing fall. • Add length of D-ring Extension if applicable. ∆ Printed Copy is for reference only. Refer to the CCS Safety SharePoint site for the most current version. Page 16 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 5.3.8 When calculating actual distance, make necessary adjustments based on job set-up and equipment used. Page 17 of 29 Fall Prevention & Protection Standard 5.3.11 Use caution while wearing a body harness and lanyard when you are not tied off. Wear the lanyard in such a manner that it will not get caught on or in equipment, structures or moving machinery. No part of the lanyard shall be allowed to dangle freely when not tied off. The lanyard shall be secured tightly against the user's body. • All adjustment straps of the body harness shall be kept snug against the body. 5.3.12 Where movement beyond the reach of a single lanyard (including SRLs) is required, a hazard assessment must be conducted to determine the need for lifelines and dual SRLs with twin-leg lanyards. Where dual SRLs with twin-leg lanyards are required, the person must ensure that at least one leg is attached all the time insuring 100% tie-off. 5.3.13 Self-retractable lifeline/lanyards shall never be wrapped back into themselves unless designed to do so. Wrapping can weaken the lanyard by 75 percent. Instead, beam wraps and other engineered devices shall be used. 5.3.14 The use of beam straps, beam clamps, or other temporary tie-off points must be used exclusively as part of a fall protection system and not used for rigging, lifting or other purposes. All anchor points must meet at least 5,000-pound tensile strength requirements and be designed and used for the intended purpose. EXAMPLES Printed Copy is for reference only.
